Quantum complementarity
Davide Rolino, Paolo Perinotti, Alessandro Tosini

Abstract
We propose an operational definition of complementarity, pinning down the concept originally introduced by Bohr. Two properties of a system are considered complementary if they cannot be simultaneously well defined. We further show that, within quantum theory, this notion is equivalent to the incompatibility of operations—that is, their inability to be performed simultaneously.



In the early days of quantum mechanics, some counterintuitive features of the theory
were not yet thoroughly formalised, and physicist struggled to grasp them in the
classical language. A paradigmatic example is that of Bohr’s concept of
complementarity

Bohr 1928
;
Bohr 1935
;
Pauli 1980
;
Saunders 2005
. A prominent manifestation of complementarity is the so-called wave-particle duality—a simplified phrasing for the observation that neither the corpuscular nor the wave-like behaviour of a quantum particle fully capture its physics. In the modern language of quantum field theory, a thorough mathematical formalism allows one to encompass both of these complementary aspects of microscopic systems, however, the interpretative challenges are only shifted to a different level.

Following Bohr’s original idea, an extensive line of research has developed around wave-particle
duality
Englert 1996
;
Scully et al. 1991
;
Jaeger et al. 1995
;
Dürr et al. 1998
;
Bimonte and Musto 2003
;
Martinez-Linares and Harmin 2004
;
Schilling and von
Zanthier 2012
;
De Zela 2013
;
Prabhu Tej et al. 2014
;
Kraus 1987
;
Busch et al. 1995
;
Busch and Lahti 1995
;
Busch et al. 2007
;
Petz 2007
;
Plotnitsky 2014
;
Saha et al. 2020
;
Hsieh et al. 2023
;
Serino et al. 2024
. Traditionally, complementarity is a notion associated to
physical quantities
that can be measured on a quantum system
Kraus 1987
;
Busch et al. 1995
;
Busch and Lahti 1995
;
Busch et al. 2007
;
Petz 2007
;
Plotnitsky 2014
;
Saha et al. 2020
;
Hsieh et al. 2023
;
Serino et al. 2024
.

However, in the context of quantum information, the physical interpretation of measurement outcomes plays a secondary role, and a notion such as complementarity must be defined in a more abstract manner.
In this Letter, we address this question by defining complementarity in terms of the impossibility of simultaneously assigning truth values to statements concerning the outcomes of particular measurements, which we deem
properties
of a physical system. These properties are characterised not only by their statistical features but also by their dynamical effects. We then show that complementarity is equivalent to the incompatibility of the instruments associated with the measurement of such properties. Moreover, we demonstrate that complementary measurements are incompatible also in general theories of information processing
Hardy 1999
;
Barnum et al. 2006
;
Barrett 2007
;
Spekkens 2007
;
Chiribella et al. 2010
;
Janotta and Lal 2013
;
D’Ariano et al. 2016
;
Plávala 2023
.

Following the literature, the notion of complementarity regards
properties
of a physical system. Since a property is some predicate of a system that has to exhibit some degree of objectivity—or better, intersubjectivity—in its very definition we will not only require that its values might have truth values (corresponding to probability 0 or 1) but also that a repeated measurement would not provide contradictory assertions. Actually, the former feature can be derived as a consequence of the latter. In this way, the dynamical aspects become important in the distinction between any measurement and the measurement of a property. Once we have a satisfactory definition at hand, we introduce the notion of
complementary
properties. This notion captures the idea that there is a trade-off between the truth values of statements about the value of different properties: if one has definite truth values, those of the other need to be undefined.

More formally, let
\mathrm{A}
be a quantum system with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}{}
, and let
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}=\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
be a
quantum instrument
for
\mathrm{A}
, that is a collection of
quantum operations

\mathscr{T}_{x}
, i.e. Completely Positive Trace Non-Increasing (CPTNI) maps, such that their sum is a
channel
, i.e. a Completely Positive Trace-Preserving (CPTP) map.
We will also represent instruments and quantum operations graphically via wired boxes

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise where input and output wires depict the input and output systems.

In particular, we will consider instruments with single-Kraus quantum operations, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{x}\left({\rho}\right)=T_{x}{\rho}T_{x}^{\dagger},\quad\forall x\in\mathsf{X}

(1)

We recall that single Kraus quantum operations
\mathscr{T}\left(\cdot\right)=T\cdot T^{\dagger}
belong to the extremal rays of the cone of Completely Positive (CP) maps. Therefore, they cannot be decomposed as a coarse graining (sum) of other operations. The only allowed decomposition is of the form:
\mathscr{T}=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{X}}{p}_{x}\mathscr{T}
, where
\left\{{p}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
is a probability distribution. Following the literature on operational theories, we refer to these transformations as
atomic operations
, and an
atomic instrument
as a collection of atomic operations.

We now define an instrument
repeatable
if applying it multiple times to the same system is equivalent to applying it once. A Stern-Gerlach apparatus is a typical example: repeated applications with the same magnetic field orientation will provide the same outcome. Formally, this requirement is expressed as
D’Ariano et al. 2016
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{x}\mathscr{T}_{x^{\prime}}=\delta_{x,x^{\prime}}\mathscr{T}_{x}\quad\forall x,x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}.

(2)

We now define
elementary properties
as repeatable atomic quantum instruments.
Clearly, thanks to condition (
2
), each event admits a state
\rho^{\left(x\right)}
that occurs with probability one:
\mathsf{Tr}[\mathscr{T}_{x}\left(\rho^{\left(x\right)}\right)]=1
, which can be obtained as
\rho^{\left(x\right)}=\mathscr{T}_{x}\left(\rho\right)/\mathsf{Tr}[\mathscr{T}_{x}\left(\rho\right)]
for any state
\rho
.
We refer to such a state as a
verifier
for the event
\mathscr{T}_{x}
, and more generally, for the property
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}
. We denote the set of verifiers of the operation
\mathscr{T}_{x}
as
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathscr{T}_{x}\right)
and that of the property
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}
as
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\right)
, respectively. We can also introduce the notion of a
property
, which differs from that of elementary property in that it can be obtained as a coarse graining of an elementary property, where one or more outcomes can be obtained by merging two or more outcomes of the elementary property, e.g.
\mathscr{T}_{x}=\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{y_{1}}+\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{y_{2}}
.

In the case of quantum theory, it is possible to characterise instruments that correspond to elementary properties. Consider a repeatable atomic instrument as in Eq. (
1
). From the repeatability condition, it follows that

\displaystyle T_{x}^{2}=e^{i\phi}T_{x}\>\Rightarrow\>T_{x}=e^{i\phi}\Pi_{x},

(3)

where
\Pi_{x}
is the projection on the support of
T_{x}
, as can be shown
applying the pseudoinverse of
T_{x}
to both sides of the first equation.
The global phase factor plays no role, since Kraus operators appear only in expressions of the form
T_{x}{\rho}T_{x}^{\dagger}
, hence we ignore it. Therefore, for an atomic repeatable instrument we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{x}(\rho)=\Pi_{x}\rho\Pi_{x}.

Moreover, given two such operations in the same instrument, by condition (
2
) we have
{\Pi}_{x}{\Pi}_{x^{\prime}}=0
. This implies that elementary properties of quantum theory have the form

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{x}(\rho)=\Pi_{x}\rho\Pi_{x},\quad\Pi_{x}\Pi_{x^{\prime}}=\delta_{xx^{\prime}}\Pi_{x},\quad\sum_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\Pi_{x}=I.

(4)

Having characterised the elementary properties of quantum theory, we are now in position to define two elementary properties as complementary if there exists a verifier state for one that is not a verifier state for the other. If we prepare such a verifier state, right after the measurement of the corresponding property we have a situation where the verified property is well-defined, while the other is undefined. This cannot arise classically: the pure states of a classical system are identified with the verifiers of the unique elementary property (point in the phase space) which is thus well-defined.

We remark that in the definition of complementarity we consider elementary properties. This follows from an operational
desideratum
: complementarity must be unrelated to the noise introduced in the measurement by ignoring features through coarse-graining events. Consider, for example, the following two non-elementary properties for a qutrit

\displaystyle\left\{\Pi_{0}\cdot\Pi_{0}+\Pi_{1}\cdot\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}\cdot\Pi_{2}\right\},

\displaystyle\left\{\Pi_{+}\cdot\Pi_{+}+\Pi_{-}\cdot\Pi_{-},\Pi_{2}\cdot\Pi_{2}\right\},

with
\Pi_{i}=\left|{i}\right\rangle\left\langle{i}\right|
, and
\{\left|{i}\right\rangle\}_{i=0,1,2}
denotes the canonical basis while
\left|{\pm}\right\rangle=(\left|{0}\right\rangle\pm\left|{1}\right\rangle)/\sqrt{2}
. It is immediate to see that these two properties have all verifier states in common. However, this is not completely satisfactory: complementarity does not arise only because information from a more refined measurement is ignored. Non-complementarity of this kind is not of interest, given that it arises only due to coarse graining.

We will now introduce three decreasingly strong definitions of complementarity.
Consider two complementary elementary properties
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
and
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}
. Suppose that
\rho
is a verifier state for
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}
. The first definition requires that
\mathsf{Tr}[\mathscr{G}_{y}\rho]=\frac{1}{|\mathsf{Y}|}
for all
y\in\mathsf{Y}
, where
|\mathsf{Y}|
is the cardinality of
\mathsf{Y}
. In this case we say that the two properties are
strongly complementary

1
1

1

This definition of complementarity is aligned with the one proposed in Ref.
Kraus 1987
.
, since the property
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}
is maximally undetermined if the physical system is prepared in the state
\rho
. The second definition requires that
\mathsf{Tr}[\mathscr{G}_{y}\rho]\in(0,1)
for all
y\in\mathsf{Y}
, in which case we deem the properties
mildly complementary
. Here the property
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}
is undetermined but its outcomes can occur with unbalanced probabilities, provided they are not vanishing. In the third definition, the two properties as
weakly complementary
if
\mathsf{Tr}[\mathscr{G}_{y}\rho]\in[0,1)
for all
y\in\mathsf{Y}
. In this case we allow for events with zero probability to occur, provided that no event is certain: one can have a partial removal of indefiniteness in the second property.

The notion of
incompatibility

D’Ariano et al. 2022
of measurements and channels has been largely investigated in the literature
Busch et al. 2013
;
Heinosaari 2016
;
Heinosaari et al. 2016
and recently adapted to instruments in Refs.
D’Ariano et al. 2022
;
Buscemi et al. 2023
. In the case of instruments, we are not only interested in determining whether two operations can be performed simultaneously, but if implementing one prevents in some way a subsequent implementation of the other. Indeed one has to recover not only the classical outcomes of both measurements but also the corresponding reduced quantum states through generalised post-processings. We term this notion of incompatibility
strong incompatibility
, or, equivalently,
weak compatibility
. The notion of weak compatibility is based on the property of
exclusion
. Formally, we say that an operation
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
does not excludes the operation
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}
if it is possible to implement the first operation in such a way that it is possible to post-process the outcome so that comprehensively the second operation is implemented. In formulae this is expressed by the fact that the following equalities are satisfied

x,

(5)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern (6)

for some instrument
\left\{\mathscr{C}_{z}\right\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{BE}\right)}
and a post-processing, i.e. a family of instruments
\{\{\mathscr{P}^{\left(z\right)}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\subset\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{BE}\!\to\!\mathrm{C}\right)}
. Then, two operations are weakly compatible if the this relation is satisfied also exchanging the roles of
\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
and
\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}
, possibly for different instrument and post-processing.
In the above realization scheme an arbitrary ancillary system
\mathrm{E}
is allowed, which is later discarded via the partial trace (the symbol
e
at output wires
\mathrm{E}
).

To get a more intuitive idea of the notion of incompatibility, one can think about how the process of measuring the position of a particle changes its momentum, for example, as proposed in Heisenberg’s
gedankenexperiment

Heisenberg 1927
. Measuring the position alters the momentum of the particle, making it impossible to perform a measurement that simulates a momentum measurement on the particle
before the position measurement
. We highlight that in the case of quantum theory all quantum channels are compatible, i.e. application of one channel is not incompatible with a subsequent post-processing that simulates another channel, exploiting the fact that all channels have a unitary dilation. Incompatibility, on the other hand, is manifest in quantum instruments
D’Ariano et al. 2022
.

We now state our main result: in quantum theory complementarity is equivalent to incompatibility. The proof of this statement, whose argument is sketched in the following, can be found in the Supplementary Material.

We start looking at the implication: compatibility implies non-complementarity, namely if two elementary properties, say
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
and
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}
, are compatible then they have the same set of verifier states. In particular, the result follows from the fact that if
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}
does not exclude
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}
, then
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\right)\subseteq\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\right)
. In order to prove this latter relationship is sufficient to show that if
\rho
is a verifier state for
\mathscr{G}_{y}
, then
\mathsf{Tr}[\mathscr{G}_{y}\rho]\leq\mathsf{Tr}[\mathscr{T}_{x}\rho]
. Since the quantum operations of elementary properties are atomic, the compatibility condition (
6
) gives

One can then notice that
\sum_{z\in\mathsf{Z}^{\left(x\right)}}\mathsf{Tr}[\mathscr{P}^{\left(z\right)}_{y}\mathscr{C}_{z}\rho]\leq\sum_{z\in\mathsf{Z}^{\left(x\right)}}\mathsf{Tr}[\mathscr{C}_{z}\rho]=\mathsf{Tr}[\mathscr{T}_{x}\rho]
, where first bound follows from the fact that
\mathscr{P}^{\left(z\right)}_{y}
are generally not deterministic. We have, therefore, recovered the desired inequality, showing that if two properties are weakly compatible, they must also be non-complementary. The above argument actually holds for all causal theories of information processing (see Supplementary Material).

We now prove the converse statement, namely that in quantum theory
non-complementarity implies compatibility
2
2

2

DR - NOTE: Lo rimuoverei

More precisely, the result extends to any theory of information processing that satisfies the properties required for the proof. However, these properties do not define a well-structured class of theories, and due to the specificity of the assumptions involved, we do not explore such generalisations further in this manuscript.
. The key to prove this result is in the form of quantum elementary properties (
3
), that generally does not hold for a generic information theory. In particular, the proof revolves around the fact that the set of verifier states
\rho^{(x)}\in\Upsilon\!\left(\mathscr{T}_{x}\right)
of any quantum elementary property
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
is such that
\mathscr{T}_{x}({\rho^{\left(x\right)}})={\rho^{\left(x\right)}}
. In words, all quantum verifiers are fixed points of the property. This follows joining the verifier condition
\mathsf{Tr}[\mathscr{T}_{x}(\rho^{\left(x\right)})]=1
and the fact that
\mathscr{T}_{x}
is projection, whose support has then to include the state
\rho^{\left(x\right)}
. Notice that the converse is always true: fixed points of the property are verifiers.

Taking for granted the above behaviour of quantum verifiers—which can be checked by direct calculation—we consider two non-complementary properties

\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
and show that they must
correspond to the same operation, up to a permutation of their elements. As a consequence they will trivially be compatible. The two instruments share the same verifier set by hypothesis:
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\right)=\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\right)
. Furthermore, for any
x\in X
, there must exist
y\in Y
such that
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathscr{T}_{x}\right)=\Upsilon\!\left(\mathscr{G}_{y}\right)
, otherwise the equality of the global verifier would be violated. Suppose now that two states in
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathscr{T}_{x}\right)
belong to
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathscr{G}_{y}\right)
and
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathscr{G}_{y^{\prime}}\right)
, respectively, with
y\neq y^{\prime}
. Then, their convex combination would belong to
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\right)
, but not to
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\right)
, which contradicts the hypothesis. By the same argument the cardinalities of
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{Y}
must be the same. Exploiting this and the fact that the Kraus operators are orthogonal projections decomposing identity we conclude that the supports of such projections must be the same for the two properties—their supports being those of joint verifier states. Since in quantum theory an orthogonal projector is uniquely determined by its support, we obtain

\Pi^{\mathsf{P}}_{x}=\Pi^{\mathsf{Q}}_{\varphi(x)}
,
for a bijection
\varphi:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
.
Hence the two instruments coincide.

Summarizing, we have shown that if two elementary properties in quantum theory are non-complementary, they must be the same property. This trivially implies that the two properties are compatible
—
also according to the
usual text-book definition of compatibility for observables, i.e. commutativity of their Positive-valued Measures (PVMs)
Busch et al. 2013
;
Heinosaari 2016
;
Heinosaari et al. 2016
;
Buscemi et al. 2023
.

In this Letter  we fomalized in an information theoretic setting the notion of complementarity originally introduced by Born. Building on this informational definition—grounded in the concept of properties, understood as ensembles of repeatable atomic operations—we provided a characterization of non-complementary properties in quantum theory. Therefore, it has been proved the equivalence between complementarity and incompatibility of quantum operations. The study of the relationship between the two dates back to the origins of quantum mechanics, in the debate between Bohr and Heisenberg. In the Supplementary Material we generalize the present notions from quantum mechanics to an arbitrary information theory and show that the implication incompatibility
\Rightarrow
complementarity always holds. The opposite implication is instead rooted in the characterization of verifiers as invariant states under the action of an elementary property.

As a future perspective it would be relevant to understand complementarity as a resource and better comprehend how the measure of complementarity here proposed could be related to the measures of incompatibility known in the literature
Buscemi et al. 2023
.

It would also be interesting to investigate the connection of complementarity with classicality—in the operational framework by us considered captured by the notion of simpliciality
D’Ariano et al. 2020
—in order to assess whether complementarity could provide a suitable notion to distinguish the quantum from the classical world.

Appendix A
Operational Probabilistic Theories

We aim here at giving a brief introduction to the framework of Operational Probabilistic Theories (OPTs)
Chiribella et al. 2010
;
Chiribella et al. 2011
;
D’Ariano et al. 2016
;
Chiribella et al. 2016
;
D’Ariano et al. 2020
;
Perinotti 2020
in order to prove that the result that complementarity implies incompatibility, discussed in the main text, can be generalised also to generic theories of information processing.

The purpose of the framework is to be able to describe and study all theories that have a compositional structure analogous to that of quantum (and classical) theory. Indeed the way physical processes can be composed sequentially or in parallel is the same for all these theories. One of the main objectives of the framework is to classify these theories with respect to the possibility or impossibility of performing specific information processing tasks, e.g., the possibility of making simultaneous statements about properties of physical systems and studying logical dependencies among such features.

The construction of an Operational Probabilistic Theory is based on three fundamental concepts: systems, instruments and transformations, and probabilities.

Systems
, or better
system types
, symbolically denoted by capital Roman letters
\mathrm{A}
,
\mathrm{B}
,
\ldots
, are an abstract notion that is used to keep track of causal connections between physical events. One example of a system type is a mode of the electromagnetic field, or a mode of the Dirac field, or, in non-relativistic quantum mechanics, the electron spin, or a system of two electrons.

Instruments
,
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}
, instead, model the experiments or processes that can occur to systems. Every instruments has an input system
\mathrm{A}
and an output system
\mathrm{B}
, while

\mathsf{X}
is the
outcome space
associated to the experiment, which is the set of all possible classical outcomes that can be registered as a consequence of running the experiment. Each outcome corresponds to the occurrence of a particular physical event, mathematically modelled through the notion of a
transformation

\mathscr{T}_{x}\in\mathsf{Transf\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}
. In a classical setting, an instrument could, for example, model the roll of a die, with every transformation being associated to the die ending up on a particular face—the corresponding number representing the outcome of the experiment.

As previously mentioned, the characterising aspect of Operational Probabilistic Theories is their compositional structure, which aims to reproduce that of quantum theory. In particular, we require the collection of instruments, and consequently transformations, to be closed under sequential and parallel composition. Formally, this means that for every pair of instruments
\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}
,
\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{B}\!\to\!\mathrm{C}\right)}
where the output system of the former coincides with the input system of the latter, there exists an instrument
\{{\mathcal{G}_{y}}\circ{\mathcal{T}_{x}}\}_{\left(x,y\right)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{C}\right)}
in the Operational Probabilistic Theory modelling experiments subsequently performed on a physical system. Analogously, in the case of parallel composition, for every pair of instruments
\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}
,
\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{C}\!\to\!\mathrm{D}\right)}
there exists
\{{\mathcal{T}_{x}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{G}_{y}}\}_{\left(x,y\right)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Y}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{AC}\!\to\!\mathrm{BD}\right)}
describing the situation in which two independent instruments are independently performed on the corresponding subsystems.

An added value of the framework is that it allows for a graphical representation of complex networks of processes. Instruments and transformations are represented as wired boxes

40.00015pt\raise-8.00003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}=\quad\!\!\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 8.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-5.69794pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle{\mathrm{AC}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 16.00006pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.25pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.42366pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-14.42366pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt\raise-3.41666pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathscr{T}}\boxtimes{\mathscr{G}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 24.00009pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-5.80557pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle{\mathrm{BD}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.00012pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.00015pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned},

respectively.

A particular type of system that is assumed to be always present in any Operational Probabilistic Theory is the trivial system, denoted
\mathrm{I}
, which is used to describe physical processes in which there are no input and/or output systems. The trivial system acts as the identity for parallel composition of systems, meaning that
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{A}=\mathrm{A}\mathrm{I}=\mathrm{A}
for any system in the Operational Probabilistic Theory.
Physical processes where the input system is trivial are preparations,
while those where the output system is trivial are measurements, which are processes that just produce a classical output but no output system is used in subsequent instruments. Preparation-instruments will be denoted by Greek letters,
\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Prep\!\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
, while observation-instruments with lowercase Roman letters,
\{\text{a}_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Obs\!\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
. The transformations composing a preparation-instrument are called
states
and their set is denoted by
\mathsf{St\!\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
. The transformations composing observation-instruments, instead, are called
effects
and their set is represented as
\mathsf{Eff\!\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
. Diagrammatically, the trivial system will not be drawn. Preparation- and observation-instruments will then be represented as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 8.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.25pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 20.14761pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-20.14761pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 2.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vphantom{\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\rule{0.0pt}{1.00006pt}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 16.00006pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-2.9514pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 24.00009pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 32.00012pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned},

and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise respectively.

We can now discuss the last element characterising Operational Probabilistic Theories, the possibility of assigning probabilities to closed circuits. The compositional structure described so only allows to describe any experiment that can be conceived within a given theory, but it does not allow to make predictions that are comparable with real-world data, something that we deem essential to have a well defined notion of a physical theory. The way this is obtained in the framework is through the requirement that any instrument with both input and output system trivial, is a probability distribution. As an example, we have the following identification

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 8.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.25pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 20.14761pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-20.14761pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 2.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vphantom{\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\rule{0.0pt}{1.00006pt}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 16.00006pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-2.9514pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 24.00009pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.25pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 21.7582pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-21.7582pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt\raise-2.31946pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.00012pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-2.79515pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.00015pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.25pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lx@xy@svgnested{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 38.8821pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-19.44104pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 2.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.00006pt}\{\text{a}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-33.8821pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{\text{a}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-38.8821pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\{\text{a}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-38.8821pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\{\text{a}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-38.8821pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\{\text{a}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-38.8821pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\{\text{a}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.00018pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}={p}\left(x,y,z|\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}},\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}},\{\text{a}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\right),

i.e. the circuit on the left has to be interpreted as the probability of obtaining the outcomes
x,y,z
given that the experiments
\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Prep\!\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
,
\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}\in\mathsf{Transf\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}
, and
\{\text{a}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\in\mathsf{Obs\!\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
have been performed.

An important aspect of the framework that will be extensively used in our argument is that it is always possible to coarse grain results, grouping outcomes together. For every test
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
and every disjoint partition
\left\{\mathsf{Z}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}
of the outcome space
\mathsf{X}
there exists the test
\mathsf{T}^{{}^{\prime}}_{\mathsf{Y}}
representing the same operation, where the outcome
y\in\mathsf{Y}
stands for “the outcome of the test
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}
belongs to
\mathsf{Z}_{y}
”. The transformation
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{y}=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Z}_{y}}\mathscr{T}_{x}
is called
coarse-grained transformation
.
Obviously, given a test
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}
the full coarse-graining
\mathscr{T}_{\mathsf{X}}=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\mathscr{T}_{x}
is
deterministic
. We call
deterministic
a transformation associated with a test whose outcome space has just one element. A deterministic transformation does not provide information (the associated test has a unique outcome, occurring with certainty), and can represent e.g. the evolution of an open system. In quantum theory, a deterministic transformation is called a
quantum channel
.

As already mentioned in the main text, related to the notion of coarse graining, there exists a particular class of transformations, called
atomic
, that cannot be written as coarse graining of any other transformation different from themselves.

Definition 1

(Atomic transformation)
.

A transformation
\mathscr{T}\in\mathsf{Transf\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}
is
atomic
if, given
\mathscr{T}_{1}
,
\mathscr{T}_{2}\in\mathsf{Transf\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}
, one has the following implication:

\mathscr{T}=\mathscr{T}_{1}+\mathscr{T}_{2}\implies\mathscr{T}_{1},\mathscr{T}_{2}\propto\mathscr{T}.

Consequently, we also define
atomic instruments
.

Definition 2

(Atomic instrument)
.

An instrument
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
is called an
atomic instrument
if its transformations
\mathscr{T}_{x}
are atomic for all
x\in\mathsf{X}
.

We have covered al the fundamental structure of Operational Probabilistic Theories necessary to carry out our argument. However, we remark that the framework if more complex that the one presented here and for the interested reader we refer to Refs.
D’Ariano et al. 2016
;
D’Ariano et al. 2020
;
Perinotti 2020
;
Rolino et al. 2025
.

A.1
Useful definitions

Now that we have set up the basic structure of the framework of Operational Probabilistic Theories we can discuss a series of properties that can be satisfied by a theory and that are of interest for the present paper.

A.1.1
Causality

The first notion that we would like to introduce is that of
causality
.

Definition 3

(Causal Operational Probabilistic Theories)
.

A
causal Operational Probabilistic Theory

\Theta
is a theory where every system
\mathrm{A}\in\mathsf{Sys\left(\mathrm{\Theta}\right)}
admits a unique deterministic effect
D’Ariano et al. 2016
. In symbols,
\forall\mathrm{A}\in\mathsf{Sys\left(\mathrm{\Theta}\right)}
one has
\mathsf{Eff_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}=\{\text{e}\}
, where
\mathsf{Eff_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
indicates the set of deterministic effects of system
\mathrm{A}
.

The reason why this property is referred to as causality is that it can be proven to be equal to the property of
no-signalling from the future
, that is that the probability distributions of preparation-instruments does not depend on the choice of the observation-instruments at their output
D’Ariano et al. 2016
. Formally,

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}{p}\left(x,y|\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}},\{\text{a}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}\right)

\displaystyle={p}\left(x|\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}},\{\text{a}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}\right)

\displaystyle={p}\left(x|\{\rho_{x}\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\right).

Consequence of this is that in causal theories the flow of information is fixed from input to output (diagrammatically, from left to right).

A.1.2
Conditional instruments

A particular operation that can be encoded in the structure of an Operational Probabilistic Theory is the possibility of conditioning which operation to perform based on the result of a previous experiment.

Definition 4

(Conditional instruments)
.

Let
\Theta
be an Operational Probabilistic Theory,
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}=\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}
a test of the theory, and
\left\{\mathsf{G}^{\left(x\right)}_{\mathsf{Y}}=\{\mathscr{G}^{\left(x\right)}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\subset\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{B}\!\to\!\mathrm{C}\right)}
a labelled collection of instruments. In the case where the collection

\{\mathscr{T}_{(x,y)}\}\coloneqq\left\{{\mathscr{G}^{\left(x\right)}_{y}}\circ{\mathscr{T}_{x}}\right\}_{(x\times y)\in\mathsf{X\times Y}},

(7)

is a valid instrument, we call it a
conditional instrument
.

This kind of operation is sometimes referred to in the literature as
classical feedback
or
post-processing

Leppäjärvi and Sedlák 2021
.

Remark 1
.

In general the object in (
7
) may not belong to
\mathsf{Instr\left({\Theta}\right)}
, namely it may model an operation that it is not actually implementable in the theory. An example of a theory where not every conditional
instrument is an actual instrument of the theory is Minimal Classical Theory proposed in Ref.
Erba et al. 2024
. A theory where all conditioned instruments are allowed is referred to as
strongly causal

D’Ariano et al. 2016
;
Perinotti 2020
;
Rolino et al. 2025
.

A.2
Compatibility

In conclusion of our discussion on the framework of Operational Probabilistic Theories, we will provide the precise definition of the notion of weak-compatibility. As mentioned in the main text, the notion of weak-compatibility is based on the notion of exclusion between instruments.

Definition 5

(Does not exclude)
.

Let
\Theta
be a causal Operational Probabilistic Theory, we say that an instrument
\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}

does not exclude
another instrument
\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{C}\right)}
if there exists a test
\left\{\mathscr{C}_{z}\right\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{BE}\right)}
and a post-processing, i.e. a family of instruments
\left\{\{\mathscr{P}^{\left(z\right)}_{y}\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}\right\}_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\subset\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{BE}\!\to\!\mathrm{C}\right)}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise Consequently, we can define weakly-compatible instruments in the following way.

Definition 6

(Weakly-compatible instrument)
.

Let
\Theta
be a causal Operational Probabilistic Theory, we say that two instruments
\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}
and
\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{C}\right)}
are
weakly-compatible
if one does not exclude the other and viceversa.

In the particular case of observation-instruments, the compatibility condition takes a simpler form than (

Besides being a special instance of the more general definition involving arbitrary instruments, this is also the Operational Probabilistic Theories analogue of the definition of compatibility between Positive Operator-Valued Measures
Busch 2009
;
Heinosaari et al. 2016
;
Heinosaari et al. 2019
in quantum theory.

Appendix B
Complementarity in OPTs

We now formally define the notion of
complementarity
for generic theories of information processing.

Let us start by generalising the notions of repeatable instruments and verifier states, introducing also the notion of strong verifiers that occur in quantum theory.

Definition 8

(Repeatable instruments)
.

Let
\mathsf{T}=\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
, we say that
\mathsf{T}
is
repeatable
if

\mathscr{T}_{x}\mathscr{T}_{x^{\prime}}=\delta_{x,x^{\prime}}\mathscr{T}_{x}\quad\forall x,x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}.

Definition 9

(Verifier state)
.

Consider a generic Operational Probabilistic Theory and let
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}
be an instrument of the theory. We say that a state
\rho\in\mathsf{St_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{AE}\right)}
, where
\mathrm{E}
is a generic system of the theory, is a
verifier state
for the instrument, denoted
\rho\in\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\right)
, if there exist
x\in\mathsf{X}
such that

(9)

for all deterministic effects
\text{e}_{k}\in\mathsf{Eff_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{BE}\right)}
.

Given that the outcome
x\in\mathsf{X}
such that (
9
) is unique, we will also say that
\rho
is a verifier state for the particular transformation
\mathscr{T}_{x}
, denoted
\rho\in\Upsilon\!\left(\mathscr{T}_{x}\right)
.

.

\rho\in\mathsf{St_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{AE}\right)}
is a strong verifier for an instrument
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{B}\right)}
if there exists

(10)

It is immediate to see that if a state satisfies (
10
), then it also always satisfies (
9
). The converse is in general not true. Consider for example the states
|{0})
and
\frac{1}{2}\left(|{0})+|{1})\right)
for the transformation

0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{({0}|+({1}|}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-36.22229pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{({0}|+({1}|}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-36.22229pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{|{0})}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{|{0})}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.11108pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{|{0})}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 32.71106pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-2.9514pt\raise 5.11108pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.88882pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 49.06659pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}.

While both states are verifiers for the latter transformation, only the former one is a strong verifier.

Exploting those definitions we can define
elementary properties
.

Definition 10

(Elementary property)
.

Let
\mathsf{P}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{P}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
be a repeatable atomic instrument such that any transformation composing it admits a verifier state, i.e.,
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathscr{P}_{x}\right)\neq\emptyset
for all
x\in\mathsf{X}
. We denote
elementary property (of system
\mathrm{A}
)
any instrument that is like
\mathsf{P}
.

This definition summarises the two main features that we require from a property: that it can be verified under suitable circumstances (existence of verifier states), and that it holds a notion of objectivity—or better, inter-subjectivity—(repeatability).

More in general it could be possible to define a
property
for a certain system
\mathrm{A}
as any coarse graining of an elementary property.

Finally we define what does it mean for elementary properties to be
complementary
and the degrees of complementary that can arise.

Definition 11

(Complementary elementary properties)
.

Let
\mathsf{P}
,
\mathsf{P^{\prime}}
be two elementary properties. They are said to be
complementary
if there exists
\rho\in\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{P}\right)\cup\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{P^{\prime}}\right)
such that
\rho\notin\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{P}\right)\cap\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{P^{\prime}}\right)
. In words, two elementary properties are complementary if there exists a verifier state of one that is not a verifier state of the other.

If an Operational Probabilistic Theory admits complementary elementary properties, we will say that it has
complementarity
.

We would like to highlight two facts related to the definitions in this section. The first one is that the definition would hold also in the case of non-causal Operational Probabilistic Theories. We are not making any assumption on the uniqueness of the deterministic effect. The second one is that we are considering also the presence of an ancillary system
\mathrm{E}
. This is because we want our definition to take into account the possibility of Operational Probabilistic Theories that do not satisfy the property of local discriminability
D’Ariano 2006
;
Hardy and Wootters 2012
;
D’Ariano et al. 2016
;
D’Ariano et al. 2020
;
Centeno et al. 2025
. This means that to characterise the state of a multipartite system it is not sufficient to make local measurements, as it is the case in quantum and classical theory. If multiple agents are sharing a system, as for example in a Bell-like scenario where Alice and Bob share an entangled pair, then in order to characterise the system it is not sufficient to perform only measurements in the two separated laboratories, but an overall measurement of the composite system would be needed.

Different degrees of complementarity between properties can be specified, depending on how much the definition of one property undermines the knowledge of the other. Entropy provides a natural way to capture this loss of information.

Definition 12

(Degrees of complementarity)
.

Let
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P^{\prime}}\equiv\left\llbracket\mathscr{P}_{x}^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
be two complementary elementary properties, and let
\nu\in\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{P}\right)
be a verifier state of
\mathsf{P}
. For every
\text{e}_{k}\in\mathsf{Eff_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{AE}\right)}
, define the conditional probabilities

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&&\cr&&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 8.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\nu}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.00003pt\raise-6.25pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 59.93999pt\raise-6.25pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.40277pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{e}_{k}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.3046pt\raise 4.49997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cirbuild@}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.3046pt\raise-16.99997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cirbuild@}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 82.30463pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-12.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 8.00003pt\raise-12.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\nu}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 30.93988pt\raise-12.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 38.93991pt\raise-12.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-2.6875pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.93994pt\raise-12.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 54.93997pt\raise-12.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.00002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\text{e}_{k}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 82.30463pt\raise-12.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\;\coloneq\;{p}_{\nu}\!\left(x\,\middle|\,\text{e}_{k}\right),

and the corresponding Shannon entropy

\displaystyle H_{\nu}\left(\text{e}_{k}\right)\;\coloneq\;-\sum_{x\in\mathsf{X}}{p}_{\nu}\!\left(x\,\middle|\,\text{e}_{k}\right)\log\!\left({p}_{\nu}\!\left(x\,\middle|\,\text{e}_{k}\right)\right).

(11)

We say that
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P^{\prime}}
are complementary to the following degrees:

I)

Strong:

\displaystyle\min_{\text{e}_{k}\in\mathsf{Eff_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{AE}\right)}}H_{\nu}\left(\text{e}_{k}\right)=\log\!\left(|\mathsf{X}|\right).

In words, the second property is completely undermined: every outcome occurs with equal probability, independently of the effect chosen.

II)

Mild:

\displaystyle 0<\max_{\text{e}_{k}\in\mathsf{Eff_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{AE}\right)}}H_{\nu}\left(\text{e}_{k}\right)\leq\log\!\left(|\mathsf{X}|\right),

with
{p}_{\nu}\!\left(x\,\middle|\,\text{e}_{k}\right)\in(0,1)
for all
x\in\mathsf{X}
and for all
\text{e}_{k}
.
Here one has some limited information, but every outcome remains possible.

III)

Weak:

\displaystyle 0<\max_{\text{e}_{k}\in\mathsf{Eff_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{AE}\right)}}H_{\nu}\left(\text{e}_{k}\right)\leq\log\!\left(|\mathsf{X}|\right),

with
{p}_{\nu}\!\left(x\,\middle|\,\text{e}_{k}\right)\in[0,1)
for all
x\in\mathsf{X}
and for all
\text{e}_{k}
.
In this case, some outcomes are excluded entirely, hence one has partial knowledge about the second property.

We stress that the distinction between mild and weak complementarity lies in whether all outcomes remain possible, or some are strictly forbidden.

Furthermore, using the definition of the Shannon entropy introduced in
Definition 12
, one immediately obtains the following result.

Lemma 1
.

Let
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{Instr\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
be two elementary properties, and let
\nu\in\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{P}\right)
. Then
\nu
is a verifier for
\mathsf{P^{\prime}}
if and only if

\displaystyle H_{\nu}\left(\text{e}_{k}\right)=0\quad\forall\text{e}_{k}\in\mathsf{Eff_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{AE}\right)},

where the entropy
H_{\nu}\left(\text{e}_{k}\right)
is defined in (
11
).

The relations characterising the different degrees of complementarity presented in
definition

12
take a particularly simple form in the case of causal Operational Probabilistic Theories, where the deterministic effect is unique.

Lemma 2

(Degrees of complementarity, causal case)
.

Consider a causal Operational Probabilistic Theory and let
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P^{\prime}}\equiv\left\llbracket\mathscr{P}_{x}^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{x\in\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{Instr\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
be two complementary elementary properties. Let
\nu\in\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{P}\right)
, with
\nu\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69444pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{P^{\prime}}\right)
. Then, with respect to the unique deterministic effect
\text{e}\in\mathsf{Eff_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{AE}\right)}
, the state
\nu
generates complementarity between the two properties in one of the following three degrees:

I)

Strong:

Mild:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern This latter definition of the different degrees of complementarity is the one presented in the main text.

Appendix C
Relation between complementarity and incompatibility

We now prove the main result of our work, namely that for generic causal Operational Probabilistic Theories complementarity implies incompatibility. This follows as a corollary of the next theorem:

Theorem 1
.

Let
\Theta
be a causal Operational Probabilistic Theory. Consider two instruments
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{T}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathsf{X}}
,
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\equiv\left\{\mathscr{G}_{y}\right\}_{y\in\mathsf{Y}}\in\mathsf{Instr\!\left(\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{A}\right)}
such that the former is repeatable and the latter is both atomic and
\mathscr{G}_{y}\neq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{A}}
for all
y\in\mathsf{Y}
, where
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{A}\!\to\!\mathrm{A}}
denotes the null transformation for system
\mathrm{A}
. If
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}
does not exclude
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}

\left(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\rightarrow\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\right)
, then the set of verifier states of
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}
is included within that of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}
,
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\right)\subseteq\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\right)
.

Proof.

Let us start by explicitly stating the tests that characterise the relationship
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\rightarrow\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}
:

whenever

,

whenever

.

The latter equation implies that it must be
\lambda_{y,x}\in\left\{0,1\right\}
. Furthermore, for any
y\in\mathsf{Y}
there exists an unique
x\in\mathsf{X}
such that
\lambda_{y,x}=1
. To prove both the existence and the uniqueness we will proceed by contradiction starting from the former. Suppose that for some
y\in\mathsf{Y}
the constant is always zero, namely

for all

, then

where in the last step we used that, in general,
\mathscr{T}_{x}
is not a deterministic transformation. Putting everything together, one obtains that it must be

Hence,
\rho\in\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\right)
, or more precisely
\rho\in\Upsilon\!\left(\mathscr{T}_{x}\right)
. Given the arbitrariness of
\rho\in\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\right)
, one obtains that
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Y}}\right)\subseteq\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{X}}\right)
.
∎

Corollary 1
.

In causal Operational Probabilistic Theories complementary elementary properties are strongly incompatible, or, equivalently, weakly compatible elementary properties are non-complementary.

Proof.

Considering two elementary properties
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{X}}
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Y}}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Instr\left(\mathrm{A}\right)}
, if one supposes that they are weakly compatible, then
Theorem 1
guarantees that
\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{X}}\right)\equiv\Upsilon\!\left(\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{Y}}\right)
, i.e. that these two elementary properties are non-complementary.
∎
